The man on the other side was not told any of it either; he simply grew up with it. There is no order that fixes that and no scout to blame, and it is being charged for now in the ordinary way, a few yards at a time. Not the `bc_field_*` templates, which ask what the ground is *made* of and get the same answer for both armies — this is about who grew up being told where the water is. The knight half is `entrenched`/`down`, and the `bc_shieldwall` grant is the argument rather than a flourish: in a country like this one, running is not a direction. Every treeline, ford and sunken lane for a day in any direction is known to the men trying to kill him and unknown to him, so the ground behind the line is not an escape, it is the same problem with fewer people in it. The shield stays up and the whole miserable business gets done standing, because standing is the only version of the afternoon that has ever worked for anybody in his position, and every man along the stretch has worked that out separately without a word about it. Not `bc_knight_there_is_no_second_host`, which is a man who has stopped expecting help; this is a man who has worked out that the map is on the other side. Neither desc may name the county, a village, a river, a keep or a road; the first may not have him regret taking the place, blame a scout or a guide, mention a siege or a garrison or a burning, or solve it — the missing information cannot be acquired between now and dusk — and may not invent a number of days the engine will not tell it. The second may not have him hate the people who live here and may not put a single one of them on the page — no farmer, no burnt roof, no child at a treeline; the country does the work and the country is empty in it. Neither may use the words *enemy country*, *invader*, *occupied* or *home ground* | 2 templates, base 50, 80 |
| what was chosen for him, and what he chose | `has_lifestyle` returned **zero over the whole mod**. Batch eighty-one read *education*, which is a decision taken about a boy of eight by adults with their own reasons and closed for good by sixteen; a lifestyle is the other half of the same man — what he does with his own hours once the hours are his — and the engine tracks it separately for exactly that reason. **Neither gate reads a lifestyle on its own, and that is the whole design.** A bare `has_lifestyle = martial_lifestyle` on a commander would fire for a very large share of the men who command anything, and a gate that fires for nearly everybody is not a gate. What is worth reading is the *disagreement* between the two fields: the boy who was pointed at war and walked somewhere else, and the boy who was pointed anywhere else and walked back. **The two are disjoint by construction and on both rows** — each row of one is the exact negation of the corresponding row of the other, so no man can answer yes to both. Neither is a subset of the batch eighty-one gates: those read a learning education and an intrigue education with nothing else attached, and the first of these excludes both by requiring a martial education while the second includes both *and* stewardship and diplomacy and then narrows all four by a row they never had. `has_education_martial_trigger` is vanilla's (`00_scripted_triggers.txt:45`), the same wrapper batch eighty-one used, and it is level-agnostic on purpose — the question is what the boy was pointed at, not how well it took. The pair is the **mirror of batch eighty-one's pair**: that one put the man raised for books on the losing side and the man raised for the dark on the winning one, both about an upbringing meeting a field; these two are about an upbringing being *overruled* by twenty years of a man's own afternoons. The commander half is `adapted`/`up`. Everything going well this afternoon is going well for reasons that are not soldier's reasons — something held back that nobody asked him to hold back, four things done in the order that means none of them is done twice, knowing without checking what is left. All of it was in him before the horns went and none of it was put there by the men who decided once what he was going to be, and the strange part is how little of what he was taught he has needed. Not `bc_taught_to_do_this`, and neither gate contains the other: that reads `education_martial_4` alone at `ordered`/`up` — the best possible training doing exactly what it was meant to do — while this takes a martial education of any grade and adds the row that one never had. A martial_4 in the yard answers yes there and no here; a martial_1 over his accounts answers no there and yes here. The knight half is `bloodied`/`down` and the `bc_bloodied` grant is the argument: he *chose* this, some year in his twenties, with no tutor and nobody's blessing, and taught himself far enough to be standing where he is standing. That was never the doubtful part. What he does not have is the seven-year-old's fifteen thousand repetitions, and there is a specific hour in a losing fight when that stops being coverable with will — the block correct and a hair late, the shield back to somewhere reasonable rather than to the one place — and the cost goes onto the army's account through him. Not `bc_knight_there_is_no_seam_in_a_shieldwall`, which is intrigue-raised, winning, and a man setting habits *down*; this is a man who picked his habits up on purpose, twenty years too late to get them free. Neither desc may name a lifestyle, a focus, a perk or a tree, may put a tutor or master-at-arms on the page, or may use the words *natural*, *gift*, *born to it*, *vocation* or *calling*. The first may not name the thing he does instead — no ledgers, no books, no hawks, no chamber — because the point is that the habits arrive without their origin attached, and may not let him be pleased with himself. The second may not name an age, a year or a number of years, may not name the trade he came from, may not have him regret the choice, and may not have another man notice him at all | 2 templates, base 50, 80 |
| the household, as opposed to the levy | Every court-position keyword returned **zero over the whole mod**. Ninety-one batches read blood, oath, land, faith, tongue, crime, upbringing and wages-by-the-company, and never once read the plainest arrangement a hall contains: a man on somebody's payroll who lives in his rooms. It is not vassalage, not kinship and not a mercenary contract — it is employment, it is renewed rather than inherited, and it puts a particular kind of man in a line. **`any_court_position_employer` rather than `has_court_position`**: the latter needs a position key and a hand-written list would go stale the moment AGOT adds a post (AGOT ships its own set on top of vanilla's), while the iterator asks the question the moments actually care about — not *which* office but *whose* — and needs no key at all. Vanilla precedent for the exact form is `ep2_wedding_events.txt:18540`; AGOT uses the same iterator at `00_agot_nightswatch_values.txt:26`. Neither gate is a subset of `bc_side_has_kin_knight_trigger`: that reads `is_close_family_of`, which is blood and marriage, and a hired huntsman is not close family of the man who hires him while a brother in the knight list is not on anybody's wages. The commander gate needs no `exists` guard for the reason recorded on the kin trigger — `any_side_knight` is an iterator, not a link — and the knight gate does need one, because a side can be leaderless; `root` inside the two nested iterations, same rule as the kin trigger. A man cannot hold a court position under himself, so the commander appearing in his own knight list cannot cause a self-match. The commander half is `measured`/`up`: the stretch that is winning this is the stretch he could put names to in the dark, and knowing that does not warm him, it sets him counting — which of them has a woman in his kitchens, which has been owed a raise since autumn, how few men in the world do that work properly and how long it takes to find one. The sum comes out clean, he does not much like the look of it, and he does not stop. Not `bc_his_blood_in_the_ranks`, the other moment on the kin trigger, and not `bc_they_are_not_his_smallfolk` or `bc_knight_where_the_wages_are`, the two free-company moments: a company is a contract with a body of men and ends when the contract does, while a court position is one man, in his hall, renewed. The knight half is `adapted`/`down`: every other man along the stretch has something behind him that stays where it is if today goes wrong, and what he has is an agreement, which does not survive on its own. So while it comes apart, the thing he is holding quietly stops being this hedge and these forty yards — never his and never going to be — and becomes one rider back on the slope, because everything he owns is downstream of that rider still existing at dusk. Nothing about his footing changes and he does not announce it. Not `bc_knight_where_the_wages_are`, which is free-company, winning and `wild`/`up`; the exact opposite of `bc_knight_a_hall_is_waiting_for_him`, which is a man who could be elsewhere, where this is a man for whom the elsewhere was always the same hall; and not `bc_knight_no_house_behind_him`, because `is_lowborn` is birth and a post is a contract. Neither desc may name an office, put a sum on the page, or use the words *servant*, *hireling*, *lackey* or *retainer*. The first may not have any of them speak to him, may not have him reward anybody afterwards, and may not let the accounting become a conclusion about the kind of man he is. The second may not have him look at the commander or be looked at — the whole moment happens at two hundred yards and inside one head — may not have him think the word *dismissed*, resent the arrangement, swear anything, or be brave about it | 2 templates, base 50, 80 |
| the one he buried | `any_former_spouse` returned **zero over the whole mod**. Batch eighty-eight read the marriage a man is currently inside — a wife on the far side, a child on the way — and stopped there; the engine also keeps the marriages that *ended*, and in Westeros the overwhelming majority of them ended the one way, which makes that list mostly a list of the dead. **`is_alive = no` is not optional**: a former spouse can also be a living annulment or a woman sent back to her father, and the row keeps the iterator to the burials. **`is_married` does real work here that it could not do alone** — batch eighty-eight's header records why a bare `is_married = yes` is not a gate, being nearly every landed man in the game, but sitting beside a burial it stops being a census line and becomes the only question anybody actually asks about one: whether he filled the place again or left it empty. The two gates are **disjoint by construction**, splitting the widowed on exactly that row over an identical burial row, and neither is a subset of the batch eighty-eight gates — that batch's first requires a *living* spouse related to the enemy commander, which the first of these excludes outright, and its second is a pregnancy, a fact about a household that is growing rather than one that lost somebody. The commander half is `wild`/`up`. Most men of rank carry a small fitting behind the breastbone that makes them take the third-best line instead of the best one; it costs half a second and the half second is why they are still doing this in ten years. His came out some while back and was not put in again. It has nothing to do with nerve, nobody out here knows the first thing about his household, and what it means in practice is that when the far side opens at the shoulder he is already moving with no quiet conversation to have first. That is why it is working, and it is also why the horse lines will be short in the morning — the rung and the spent horses come out of one decision and arrive in the same breath, which is what `wild` is for. Not `bc_the_wrath_takes_him` or `bc_the_old_grudge`, the two `wild`/`up` moments the ledger comment names: both of those are a man carried by something hot, and this is the absence of something cold. Not `bc_the_heir_is_a_child`, which reads what his death costs the realm and makes him careful — this reads what it costs one household, finds the household already gone, and makes him quick. The knight half is `ordered`/`down`: he has been through it once and then built the entire apparatus a second time, so he is the only man on the stretch who has watched from the inside as a working house stops working, and nobody plans for the second week. The afternoon comes apart and he does the least stirring thing available — back one pace when the pace is called, shoulder touching the shoulder beside him, a flat refusal of every chance the day offers to make itself memorable. The rank goes backwards and does not open, and a `bc_pursuit` grant for that would be a lie about the afternoon, which is why `ordered`/`down` hands out nothing. Not `bc_knight_the_man_who_is_the_name`, which is about rank inside a family; not `bc_knight_a_hall_is_waiting_for_him`, because titles waiting are a reason a man could leave while a household depending on him is a reason he cannot afford a gesture. Neither desc may name a wife, say how she died or how long ago, put a child on the page, have him picture her, or use the words *grief*, *mourning*, *widower*, *lonely* or *heartbroken*. The first may not be read as courage, may not have him wish for or seek death, and may not make him tragic or admirable; the second may not have him make a promise, be afraid, be thanked, call the second marriage a replacement, or be bitter about having built it all again | 2 templates, base 50, 80 |
| the paper that is still good | `has_truce` returned **zero over the whole mod**. Ninety-three batches read what two men are to each other by blood, faith, tongue, oath, land and wages, and never once read the one thing they may have actually *signed*. A truce is the only bond in the game that is a document: it has a term, it was agreed to in a specific year over a specific defeat, and it is still in force this afternoon. **It is not a contradiction for this to fire** — a truce stops a man *declaring* a war and does nothing about a war a third man declared and called them both into on opposite sides, which is the whole moment. **One trigger serves both pools**, because it reads the scoped character against the far side's commander and never touches `root` — the same property that makes `bc_the_ground_belongs_to_the_far_side_trigger` usable from the knight file, and there is no second trigger here because there would be nothing different in it. The standard guard applies (`enemy_side.side_commander` is optional), and `enemy_side` rather than `var:bc_battle_foe` for the usual reason: the variable does not exist during a battle's first `is_valid`. **The direction is the scoped man's** — the API is explicit that truces are one way, asking whether the *scoped* character cannot attack the target — which is correct for both moments, since each is about a man keeping something he agreed to. The commander half is `measured`/`up`: there is a folded thing with two seals on it in a chest at home, it has years left, and nothing on this field touches it. The fighting is entirely real and so is the agreement, and holding both in one head produces a very practical set of limits now the day is going his way. He can have the field; he cannot have the afternoon that would feel best, because that is the one he would be explaining across a table next spring under those same seals to the man he took it from. It stops being a question of temper and becomes a question of what is worth the table. Not `bc_the_old_grudge` or anything on the nemesis relation — those are a history of blood and are hot, where a truce is what got *written down* after the blood — and not the bond-ladder moments, which read what two men **are** to each other rather than what they agreed. The knight half is `bloodied`/`down` on the same gate with a completely different argument: the paper is *his*, which means there was a war and he was on the wrong end of it and sat somewhere dry-mouthed while the terms were read. Every other man along the stretch is guessing what the far side will do; he is not guessing and it is worth nothing to him. He knows how that host moves once it has the shoulder, what it does with a flank that has started to bend, how long it keeps coming past the point a reasonable host would stop — and none of it changes anything he can do about his own forty yards. Where the men beside him are learning today as it happens, he is watching an itemised list he has seen before being read out again. Not `bc_knight_the_old_wound` or `bc_knight_the_old_sword`, which are about a man who has been in battles; this is a man who has been in *this* battle, against *this* host, and signed the ending of it. The pools roll separately, so no field can produce both moments. Neither desc may quote the document, name the war, give a year, or have the two men see each other, meet or parley — a battle is too far away to recognise a face — and neither may use the words *irony*, *absurd*, *farce* or *sworn enemy*, the last being exactly what neither man is. The first may not consider breaking it, be merciful in so many words, or turn the restraint into a virtue; the second may not describe the earlier battle, say what the terms were, warn anybody, be resigned to dying, or be right about the future in any specific way — he recognises a shape, he does not have information | 2 templates, base 50, 80 |
| what the noise on the far side means | Every language keyword returned **zero over the whole mod**. Ninety-four batches read culture six ways — the same culture, the westerosi/essosi split, the wildling flag, the sea between them — and never once read the layer underneath it, the one that decides whether a man can *understand* the field he is standing on. AGOT ships twenty-six languages of its own in `00_agot_language.txt`. **The two axes genuinely cross**, which is the whole reason this is worth reading separately: two Westerosi cultures that share nothing else share `language_agot_westeros`, so the countryman gate can be false while the tongue is shared, and `language_agot_first_men` exists, so two men on the same side of the sea can have no word in common. **`knows_court_language_of` rather than `knows_language` or `knows_language_of_culture`**: the first needs a language key and a hand-written list would rot the first time AGOT added one, the second reads a fact about a people rather than about these two men, and this one asks the question a battle actually poses — whether *this* man can follow the tongue *that* hall gives its orders in — and takes a character, so it needs no key. Vanilla precedent at `00_alliance.txt:659` and `:745`, both with a scope argument, and AGOT overrides that file and keeps both lines. **Court language is the right one for a battle**: a landless knight's court language is the language of the hall he belongs to, which is exactly the language the orders around him are shouted in. The two gates are disjoint by construction on the knowing row, and the second adds the culture row, which makes it disjoint from `bc_foe_is_countryman_trigger` too; the second reads `root.culture` from inside an entered scope, same form and same reason as that trigger, and root is the right man in both pools. The commander half is `adapted`/`down`. Every man who has ever run a field listens to the other host without deciding to — the correction shouted twice, which says a line is being dressed and not re-formed; one name repeated, which says who has gone down; the moment the noise stops being aimed at anybody and becomes men speaking to the men beside them. He has had that for nothing his whole life and today he has none of it. What crosses the open ground arrives with as much in it as rain on a roof, so the afternoon is being run on sight, half the instrument, on the day it is going wrong. Not `bc_the_wrong_war_entirely` or any culture template, which read who the far side *are*; not `bc_eleven_days_of_hearsay`, the other `adapted`/`down` moment on a missing fact, because that information is obtainable in principle and just not by dusk, where this channel is shut for the whole afternoon and would be shut on any ground in the world. The knight half is `adapted`/`up`: they are from somewhere else entirely and he follows them as easily as his own rank, which he never earned and never had a use for. For most of the day it is only odd; then it turns his side's way and the oddness becomes information — an instruction given a second time, a name called in a register nobody uses for an order, a stretch of them beginning to speak to each other instead of outward. The rank in front admits nothing and he moves half a pace anyway, and he is right, and right again eleven minutes later, and he says nothing to the men on either side of him. Not `bc_knight_there_is_no_seam_in_a_shieldwall`, the other knight `adapted`/`up`, which is a man setting habits *down*; the culture row is the point rather than decoration, because understanding a countryman is not a fact about anything. Neither desc may name a language or a people, quote a word in any tongue, put an interpreter, herald, captive or deserter on the page, or use the words *barbarous*, *savage*, *gibberish* or *babble*. The first may not mishear something and act on it, may not be offered a translation, and may not solve it in any degree; the second may not tell anybody what he heard, be believed, be thanked, take a prisoner, explain where he learned it, or have the hearing be a talent | 2 templates, base 50, 80 |
| the fostering | `has_relation_guardian` and `has_relation_ward` returned **zero over the whole mod**. Ninety-five batches read blood, house, oath, wages, tongue, land and paper, and never once read the arrangement Westeros actually runs on: a boy sent to another man's hall for ten years to be made into something, kept by the engine as a named relation for as long as both men live. **The direction is the whole reason there are two gates** — vanilla stores the pair as inverses, so `has_relation_ward = X` means X was raised by the scoped man and `has_relation_guardian = X` means the scoped man was raised by X — and each gate carries the negation of the other, so they are disjoint by construction rather than by argument: ordinary play cannot make two men each other's ward, but the engine would answer both if some future content set both relations, and a gate that can be true beside its own opposite is not a gate. Standard guard on both, `enemy_side` rather than `var:bc_battle_foe`, and neither reads `root`, so either is usable from either pool. The commander half is `ordered`/`up` and is **the only moment in the mod where the pursuit modifier is not a compliment**. The far side has begun to give ground and it is being given in a pattern he owns: which foot goes first, how many paces before the second rank is called through, the one thing that always gets done a beat late under pressure because it was always done a beat late in a cold yard by a boy who was bad at it for two years and then very good. He does not wait to see where the movement ends — he calls it, and it is there, and eleven minutes later he calls it again. Nobody watching would say he looks pleased; he looks like a man doing arithmetic he did once before with the answer in front of him. A chase run by a man who knows the quarry's footwork is the cleanest and worst thing on a field, which is what earns `bc_pursuit` here. Not the kin moments — a fostering is the bond that exists precisely where blood does not — and not `bc_the_old_grudge` or `bc_knight_he_has_read_these_terms_before`, because neither of those men taught the other anything: this knowledge is not a memory of having fought him but of having **made** him, and it arrives as a prediction rather than a warning. The knight half is `entrenched`/`down`: everything clever has gone out of him at that hour, and what is left is the piece that was put in before any of the clever parts existed, below where fear reaches — where the left foot goes, what the shield does when the weight comes on, that the man beside you is the point of the entire exercise. He is not deciding to do it. The two men on either side set themselves because he did and the stretch stands a good while longer than it has any business standing, and it changes nothing about how this ends. He is also aware, in a distant and unhelpful way, that the one thing he is still doing properly is somebody else's, and that the somebody else is four hundred yards away winning. `bc_shieldwall` is the argument rather than a flourish: the lesson is doing the work in front of the man who gave it. Not `bc_knight_the_squires_best_day` — a squire is an apprenticeship in arms and a fostering is a childhood, and a boy fostered at eight and squired at fourteen answers yes to both about two different men. Neither desc may name the other man, stage the childhood as a scene, have the two of them see each other, or use the words *father*, *son*, *betrayal* or *family*, the last four being precisely what a fostering is not. The first may not be proud, spare anybody, or soften the ending; the second may not want to be seen, hate the man, forgive him, remember being struck, or let the holding save the afternoon | 2 templates, base 50, 80 |
| which way the war travelled | The war scope has been reachable since plan 1 and the mod reads three things off it — `war_days >= 730`, `is_civil_war`, `is_war_leader = root` — and in ninety-six batches never once asked the first thing anybody asks about a war, which is **who came to whom**. `is_attacker_in_war` and `is_defender_in_war` both returned zero over the whole mod. They are character triggers with a war target rather than war triggers, so the shape is the inverse of `bc_the_war_is_his_trigger`: read on the man, handed the war, AGOT precedent at `common/character_interactions/00_alliance.txt:1007` and `:3811`. The walk is `bc_war_is_long_trigger`'s exactly — `exists = scope:combat_side.combat.combat_war` as a sibling, the read nested in a `trigger_if` repeating it — because being the *target* rather than the scope does not make an absent link safe to hand to a trigger. **These are the first paired gates in four batches with no explicit `NOT`, and that is deliberate**: the attacker list and the defender list are the two halves of one object, and being written into one is what keeps a man out of the other, so the disjointness is the engine's rather than the script's. The third answer is no to both and it is the common one — a courtier, a household knight, a hired sword, a son riding with his father is in neither list while standing in the exact middle of the battle — and that silence is what keeps these off the fires-for-nearly-everybody pile. The commander half is `measured`/`up` and is the coldest reading in the file: the roads held, the grain was in, a levy raised at that point in the year is men with nothing left at home still standing in a field, and the far side is where he expected them because there was nowhere else on that stretch of country a sensible man would put them. He does not think of it as skill. It is the difference between an afternoon that happens to a man and an afternoon a man ordered a long time ago in a warm room with the shutters closed, and the second sort arrives with hardly anything in it left for him to decide — a barn going up after the timber is already paid for. Not `bc_the_war_is_his_trigger`'s moment, which asks whether he is the war leader: a vassal called into his liege's invasion is an attacker and nobody's leader, a lord defending his own seat is a leader and no kind of attacker, neither gate is a subset of the other, and ownership and direction are separate facts. Not `bc_the_word_they_will_write_it_under` either — that man is thinking about the account of the afternoon, this one is thinking about the winter before it and the afternoon is only the invoice arriving. The knight half is `adapted`/`down`, the rarest pair in the ledger, and it is the version of `adapted` that has nothing to do with terrain: what he re-reads is not the ground but the question. Most of the men beside him could be swept off the end of a bad day and nobody in any hall would say their names twice; his name is in the quarrel itself, in a small careful hand, so what is being settled out there is not whether a battle is lost. The fighting quietly turns into something else in his hands — he stops trying to win any of it and starts working out what can still be kept from them before dark, this hedge, this next hour — and he is not certain whether the thing he is protecting is the land or only the sentence that gets written about him either way. The narrowness of that gate is the whole moment: it selects the men whose own name went into the war, standing in the same rank on the same afternoon as the far larger number who merely arrived with somebody who has one. Not `bc_knight_where_the_wages_are` or `bc_they_are_not_his_smallfolk`, which are about what a man is owed and by whom; the obligation here runs the other way and has his name at the bottom of it. Not `bc_knight_the_ground_here_was_worth_something`, which re-reads the country underfoot. Neither desc may name the war, its cause, its claim, the title being fought over or any place; neither may put a date, a year or a count of days on the page; neither may quote a document, batch ninety-four owning paper; and neither may report on the war as a whole, because who is winning the afternoon is visible from a field and who is winning the war is not. The first may not be pleased with himself, may not be called clever, may not have the arrangement praised to his face, and may not have any part of it go wrong so that he can be seen recovering — nothing goes wrong, that is the entire content of the moment. The second may not name what he stands to lose, blame the man who brought him, stage leaving as an option he nobly declines, be noticed, or be sent for. Nobody is coming | 2 templates, base 50, 80 |
| what nobody here can see | Ninety-seven batches read what a man took openly, what somebody holds over him, what people think of him, and what he is related to, married into and was raised by — every one of them a fact somebody else could tell you. The engine also keeps the list of facts nobody can, and **`any_secret` and `any_known_secret` had both returned zero over the whole mod**. Two iterations and no guard: `any_secret` is a list on the character and `any_secret_knower` is a list on the secret, and the mod's variable guard covers optional *links*, not iterators — `bc_side_has_kin_knight_trigger` and `bc_he_has_another_war_trigger` are unguarded for the same reason. The second gate carries the exact negation of the first plus the row that says he has something to be silent about at all, so a man with two secrets, one out and one not, answers yes to the first and no to the second, which is correct: once anybody knows anything, the second template's afternoon is not the one he is having. **Not the hook axis.** `bc_his_lord_holds_a_hook_trigger` and `bc_he_holds_his_lords_hook_trigger` are leverage between two men both attached to this battle, and a hook is a thing being *used*; these two never look at anybody on the field, and the knower may be a septon three kingdoms away who has never once thought about doing anything with it. A hook is a transaction and a secret is a condition. Not `bc_his_own_people_have_a_ledger_trigger` either — tyranny is the sum of what a man did in the open and was not stopped from doing, and the record is the whole point of it; a secret is the other pile. Most characters carry none at all, so neither gate is the fires-for-nearly-everybody kind. The commander half is `ordered`/`down`: the line is coming back the way it should, at a pace, and he is doing that part correctly with about two thirds of himself, while the other third has worked out — not decided, worked out — that there is exactly one place the thing he has carried since a particular year exists at all, and that place is walking backwards across this field with everything else. If today finishes the way it is currently arranged, it stops existing, and he cannot get that to come out as either good or bad. Not fear: the list of men who could say it is empty. Not relief either. Not `bc_a_room_somewhere_in_tonight`, the other `ordered`/`down` moment about what tonight will be — that man is withdrawing carefully because of a person he will have to sit down in front of, and this one has nobody to sit down in front of, which is precisely the problem. The knight half is `measured`/`up` and hands out nothing, because nothing about the line changes: he does the work properly and spends a small steady part of his attention making sure the credit settles somewhere else, letting the man on his right stand forward when the thing gives, being already turned away when a rider looks along the line for who did that. It is not modesty and he has never been tempted to call it that. A name that gets written down gets read, by people who read a great many of them, and there is a man out in the world with one other thing he could set beside it. **The fifty prestige is the joke and the mod knows it** — he is paid in the one currency he is trying not to accumulate, and wanting it not to arrive is the entire moment. The argument is exactly inverse to `bc_knight_it_will_be_his_name`, which is the same cell: that man swallows credit going to somebody he cannot stand, this one arranges it. Not `bc_knight_the_chain_that_put_him_here`, which reads the arrangement that got him into the line; this is about what happens after the line, in rooms, to a name. Neither desc may say what the secret is or hint at its kind — the gate cannot tell a murder from a parentage from a faith and every narrowing word is invented — may put anybody connected to it on the page, may let the day threaten to expose anything, may have him nearly say it or be asked anything, or may confess to the reader in the last line. The first may not be relieved and may not be frightened, may not decide anything, and may not die on the page. The second may not be noticed, may not refuse an order or sandbag the work, may not be praised out loud, and may not enjoy the winning. He is winning; that is the difficulty | 2 templates, base 50, 80 |
| weight, as opposed to rank | The station batch reads `highest_held_title_tier` and `is_landed` eight times between them, and both describe what a man **holds**. Neither says anything about what he is worth to the man above him, and in a realm those are different numbers: a duke can be one of six men a king cannot govern without, or he can be a duke. `is_powerful_vassal` and `is_powerful_vassal_of` had both returned zero over ninety-eight batches. The engine's own threshold is deliberately not restated here — writing a realm-fraction of our own would be the mod inventing a constitution. The first gate is an iteration and needs no guard, for the reason recorded on `bc_side_has_kin_knight_trigger`, and uses `root` inside the entered scope for the same reason that one does. **The second gate's explicit `NOT` is the point of the batch**: a powerful vassal standing in his own liege's line is what powerful vassals are *for*, and without the negation the second gate would be the first gate's subject read from the wrong end and the batch would be one idea written twice. The guard is the standing one, and it wraps the `NOT` as well, because a `NOT` around an unguarded link read is still an unguarded link read. Not the hook axis: `bc_his_lord_holds_a_hook_trigger` and `bc_he_holds_his_lords_hook_trigger` are a specific piece of leverage between two named men, held by one and usable once, and this is structural — nobody did anything to anybody and the weight would be identical if the two men had never exchanged a word. The commander half is `adapted`/`up` and hands out nothing, because nothing about the army changes; what changes is the arrangement built on top of it. There is a place where the afternoon will be decided, it has been obvious for an hour, and standing thirty feet away with a sword and a wet surcoat is the man best able to go and decide it. He does not send him. Sending him puts him in front of everyone who will talk about today for a year, and a man that size needs no help getting larger; holding him back where somebody could remark on it does the same job from the other direction; and losing him there is a chair empty next spring that takes a year to fill. So he builds a slower version — two units that do not want to be where he puts them, a delay he would rather not have taken, a wider angle onto the same ground — and it works, and nobody will ever know the shape of the win was decided by an arithmetic that had nothing to do with the ground. Not `bc_the_leash_runs_the_other_way`, the other `adapted`/`up` moment about one man's hold on another; not `bc_side_has_kin_knight_trigger`'s moments, because blood in the ranks is a thing he feels and this is a thing he calculates, and he would calculate it about a man he liked. The knight half is `entrenched`/`down` and earns `bc_shieldwall` honestly: he is one of somebody else's great men, in a line commanded by a man who cannot summon him, cannot revoke anything from him, and will not be asked about him afterwards by anybody who matters. Every order he obeys is obeyed by choice, which sounds like freedom and is the opposite — nothing here is owed, so nothing here is covered, and the whole afternoon is being written down elsewhere as a description of what he is like, by men who are not present and will repeat it accurately for twenty years. So the one outcome he cannot have is a place that broke with his name on it. He plants himself and does not move and it is neither courage nor stubbornness; it is the one calculation available, made for rooms he will be standing in next winter. The men on either side think he is very brave, and he would not know how to begin explaining that they have it wrong. Not `bc_knight_the_leash_holds`, also `entrenched`/`down`, which is a man held in place by a specific thing another man has on him — this one is held by nothing at all, which turns out to hold better. Not `bc_knight_where_the_wages_are`: he is not being paid, and he outweighs most of the men who could offer. Neither desc may name anybody, give a title, a seat, a realm or a hall, have the two men speak or look at each other, or let anybody else notice what is being arranged. The first may not resent it, may not have the great man do anything remarkable, may not have the arrangement fail, and may not use the words *faction*, *council* or *rival*. The second may not name what he stands to lose, think a single word about the man commanding him, consider leaving, be thanked, or let the holding change how the afternoon ends | 2 templates, base 50, 80 |
| the third relation | The mod reads two of the three and had never read the third. `has_relation_friend` gates `bc_knight_for_a_friend`, `has_relation_rival` gates `bc_knight_knows_that_man` and the whole rivalry seam, and in ninety-nine batches **`has_relation_lover` never appeared once**. Marriage, kin, house, fostering, wardship, employment and wages — every bond in this setting that somebody wrote down — and the one the engine keeps that nobody writes down was still missing. Both reads also point somewhere the mod has never pointed one: every `has_relation_*` in the files is aimed at the side commander or the enemy's, and the second gate aims one **into the ranks**, at whoever happens to be standing in the same line, which is where a bond like this would actually be found. The first gate takes the standing guard on `enemy_side.side_commander`; the second is an iteration and takes none, for the reason recorded on `bc_side_has_kin_knight_trigger`, and uses `root` inside the entered scope for the same reason. The second carries the first as an explicit negation rather than repeating its body, because the engine allows more than one such relation and the two templates are about opposite arrangements of the same field — written this way the man whose bond is across the line is never also the man whose bond is beside him, and the larger fact wins the tie. Neither gate is `is_married`: `bc_he_married_into_the_other_side_trigger` is a treaty between two families that can exist between people who have met four times, and this is on no document, was arranged by nobody, and can end without a seal being broken. The commander half is `measured`/`up` and hands out nothing, and the nothing is the argument — the army is not braced, bled or chasing, and what has changed is that the man running it stopped receiving the afternoon as a run of gains and began receiving it as a list of things he is choosing, one at a time, in front of witnesses, with no way of choosing differently and no way of not knowing. The flank folds: good. The flank folds onto somebody. He does not slow down and the orders go out in the same voice they went out in at dawn, and he goes on winning it hour after hour, and there is not one part of it left that belongs only to him. **The winning row and not the losing one, deliberately** — the losing version is `bc_a_room_somewhere_in_tonight` and is already written; winning is the harder afternoon and the one this batch takes. The fifty prestige is what a winning day does to a man's standing and the template does not pretend it and the cost are on the same ledger. The knight half is `adapted`/`down`, the rarest pair in the ledger and the most literal use of `adapted` in the knight file: he reads the field and changes what he is doing, and what he changes it to is not a tactic. Somewhere off to his left, he *thinks* — it has been thinking rather than knowing for a while now — there is a part of this line that matters more than the rest of it. He could not point at it. An idea is the whole of what any man in a rank has about anything further off than his own arm, and the day is taking that idea apart at the same rate it is taking everything else apart. So he stops working on the battle, which is not much use to him any more, and works on a distance instead: closing it in the pauses, three or four paces at a time, through men moving the other way who have every right to be, taking whatever comes at him from the side because turning to answer it costs ground he cannot buy back. He does not get there. The point is that the number is smaller than it was an hour ago. Not `bc_knight_for_a_friend`, which is about serving the commander well and would be a different template if the commander were a stranger — this one would be identical. Not `bc_side_has_kin_knight_trigger`'s moments: blood in the ranks is a fact he was born into and can name to a herald. Neither desc may name the person, give their sex, house, title, age or origin — a stricter prohibition than usual, and not squeamishness: the moment is a man's attention bending around somebody, and every supplied fact makes it smaller than it is. Neither may stage a scene from before the battle, have the two of them meet, see each other, call out, send or receive anything, or let anybody else on the field know. The first may not consider stopping, sparing anybody or easing off, may not have any of that occur to him and be dismissed, may not weep, and may not enjoy any part of the day. The second may not find them, reach them, see them or learn one thing about how they are, may not get men killed on the page, may not be praised or condemned, may not end well, and may not use the word *love* | 2 templates, base 50, 80 |
| the quarrel that is waiting at home | A hundred batches of gates read this field, this war, this line, this body, this bond and this ledger — who he is standing beside and who he is looking at across the grass. **The mod had never once asked what is happening behind him.** `is_a_faction_member` and `has_targeting_faction` were both at zero occurrences, and between them they are the only two questions the engine will answer about a quarrel that is not on this field at all and does not care what happens here. The two are opposite ends of one rope: the first man has a faction pointed at him, somebody at home having written his name at the top of a page and gone round collecting signatures under it; the second man has put his own name on somebody else's page. Neither fact is visible from ten feet away on a battlefield and both are the reason a particular afternoon has to end a particular way for a particular man. The first gate carries the second as an explicit negation rather than the two being left to overlap — signing against your liege while your own vassals sign against you is not a rare arrangement here, the templates are about opposite pressures, and `is_a_faction_member` wins the tie because a signature he put there with his own hand is the nearer fact. Neither takes a guard: both are plain reads on `root`, the character the template is already rooted on, and there is no optional link in either. Neither is `bc_his_lord_holds_a_hook_trigger`, which is the near miss worth naming — a hook is one man's private leverage over another and is used or it is not, while a faction is a list of names with a stated demand attached and exists whether or not anybody ever acts on it. The commander half is `wild`/`up`, which is a rung gained and the mounts finished in the same breath, and the breath is the argument: he could stop this at the good part, and most men would be right to, and the field would go into the record as a tidy piece of work by a careful man. That is precisely the shape of afternoon he cannot use. The thing assembled at home was built slowly out of exactly that kind of tidy adequate work, item by item, each one perfectly defensible and none of them enough, and a careful win becomes the newest item on it before the horses are cool. So he sends the riders out past where riders are usually sent, and then past that, and keeps them out through the part of the day when a sensible captain calls them in, and what comes back at dusk comes back walking, finished for the season, having turned a result that could be argued with into one that cannot. The mounts are the price and he worked it out this morning. The knight half is `ordered`/`down` and hands out nothing on purpose — shape kept all the way through a thing going backwards, and no modifier, because nothing about it changes how the line performs. He signed against the man whose banner is over him and that man's army is losing, and there is no version of the next hour in which he is allowed to look like somebody watching what he wanted arrive. He is being watched by nobody in particular and by every man within reach at once, which is the ordinary condition of standing in a line, and it has never mattered before what his face was doing. It matters now: everything in front of him is the thing he put his name to at a table in a cold room two winters ago, arriving on its own, without him, at no cost to him, and if one man beside him comes to think it occurred to him then the table and the room and every name on the page go into the fire together. So he works, takes his turn forward when it is his turn, is tired at the correct hour and afraid in the correct places, and permits himself no thought about any of it until well after dark. **The second may not act on what he wants** — no slowing, hesitating, misleading, holding back a step or arranging for one thing to go wrong; that is a different template, and what this one does is exactly his job at exactly the ordinary speed, which is the hardest available thing. Neither may name the faction, its demand, anybody who signed it, or say the word on the page; neither may put a number on it — not how many signed, not what each brings, not how long it has sat there, not what it will cost, the whole point being that it has no size a man in armour can hold in his head while working. Neither may have a rider, a letter or news of any kind arrive from home during the afternoon: the pressure is entirely a thing he brought with him this morning and will take away again at dusk. The first may not doubt that he is winning or be shown as having misjudged the field — the cost is not a mistake, it is a price decided before the first charge — may not use the horses as a symbol of anything, may not address anybody, and may not look back. The second may not be suspected, may not nearly say something, and the page may not decide whether he is glad | 2 templates, base 50, 80 |
| who is allowed to put whom in a cell | The mod reads every reason two men could have to want each other dead — rivalry, kin, house, faith, hooks, tyranny, opinion, kinslaying — and had **never read the one that says one of them is allowed to keep the other**. `has_imprisonment_reason` was at zero occurrences, and it is the only trigger in the engine that answers a question about *law* between these two particular men. The answer changes what a battle is for: a field you can win is not the same field as one where the man opposite is a thing you are entitled to carry home and hold. The two gates point the same law in opposite directions — whether root may lawfully take the far commander, and whether the far commander may lawfully take root — and the engine keeps those as two separate facts. Both take the standing guard on `enemy_side.side_commander`, each carrying its own `exists` sibling and repeating it in the `trigger_if` limit; the second reads the far commander's own reasons from inside his scope and so uses `root`, not `prev`, for the reason recorded on `bc_foe_is_countryman_trigger`. The second carries the first as an explicit negation, because both are often true at once — the reasons men accumulate against each other tend to be mutual — and taking wins the tie because it is the half that decides what the whole field is doing. Neither is a hook gate and neither is `bc_foe_is_formidable_trigger`: a hook is leverage that gets spent in a conversation and formidability is an estimate of this afternoon, while this is a standing entitlement that existed on paper before anybody drew a sword and will still exist tomorrow whichever way the field goes. The commander half is `adapted`/`up`, the change of purpose being exactly what `adapted` is for: everything since noon has been aimed at making the far side come apart, and it is coming apart, and in the middle of watching it he understands that this is the wrong thing to be building. A host that comes apart scatters, and scattering is how one particular man ends the day forty miles away on a borrowed horse with his helm in a ditch — and that man is worth more tomorrow than everything else on the field put together. So the orders change and none of them sound like a change: pressure comes off the centre by a degree nobody in it will feel, the wings stop pushing and begin to lengthen, a reserve that was going to break something is walked sideways and left standing where a road runs, doing nothing, in front of men who cannot think why. He is no longer beating them; he is building a shape out of a victory he has already won, and the shape has one opening left in it, and he is minding that opening himself. The knight half is `bloodied`/`down`, the line paying for one man's arithmetic. The men to either side of him have an exit — a hand up, a name given, a week of bad food and somebody's steward riding out with a sealed bag — and it stands open for every one of them and has never once been open for him. So while the line comes apart he is not working out how to live through this; he is working out how to remain, for the next hour, an object no reasonable man would put a hand on. He goes at them wide and low and keeps going, past the point where it is any use to the line, and men beside him who would have stood are pulled forward into it and do not come out. **The stress and the `bloodied` are the same fact from two directions** and the template does not pretend otherwise. Neither desc may name the reason — not the crime, the oath broken, the year, or who ruled on it — and neither may use the words *law*, *lawful* or *legal*, because men in armour do not talk like clerks. Neither may stage the capture: nobody is taken, yields, is disarmed or is walked anywhere, and no hand closes on any bridle. Neither may put a price on anything, the whole weight of both being that the ordinary trade is not on offer. The first may not gloat, be cruel, want the man for anything the page names, give an order aloud that explains the change, be understood by his own men, be noticed by the far side, or fail. The second may not describe or imagine a cell or mention a door, a room, a chain or a key, may not decide anything — the decision was made for him by somebody else years ago — may not be heroic, thanked or admired, may not die on the page, and may not get away | 2 templates, base 50, 80 |
| the one who was preferred | The mod reads blood and had **never read preference**. It knows who is kin, whose house is whose, who fostered whom, who is a bastard, who stands to inherit and who has a child on the way — every one of them a fact a herald could look up. `num_of_relation_favorite_child` and `is_favorite_child` were both at zero occurrences, and between them they are the only place the engine records the thing families actually run on: that out of several people entitled to the same amount of love, one of them gets more of it. The two gates are the same fact from the two ends — root doing the preferring, root being preferred — and since most fathers were somebody's son the second carries the first as an explicit negation, doing the preferring winning the tie because a man who is owed something and also owes something is, on a battlefield, mostly the second thing. Neither takes a guard: both are plain reads on `root` with no optional link in either. Neither is `bc_he_stands_to_inherit_trigger` or `bc_a_child_of_his_is_on_the_way_trigger`, the near misses worth naming — inheritance is an order of names on a roll and does not care who anybody likes, a child on the way is a fact about next spring, and this is neither an order nor a date but a standing arrangement of somebody's affection that everybody in the household knows about and nobody has written anywhere. The commander half is `adapted`/`down` and hands out nothing, which is right: a change of purpose does not brace a line or bleed it. Nothing about the next hour is going to be a victory and he stops asking it to be one, and the interesting part — he notices himself doing it — is where the new instruction comes from. Not the men, whom he has spent his adult life being responsible for and would spend all of today for if today were still worth spending. Not the war, which will go on being what it is regardless of this grass. It is one small thing at the far end of a road he has not travelled in months, which is not asking him for anything and does not know today is happening. He gives no order anybody could write down as the moment he stopped trying; what he does is smaller and takes longer — a company held back that would have been fed in, a flank permitted to bend rather than bought back at the price it now costs, the road behind them checked once without turning his head. **The men around him think he is still fighting the battle.** The knight half is `wild`/`up`, a rung gained and the mounts finished in the same breath. The animal under him is better than the one under any man he has passed today and it was chosen for him, instead of being chosen for somebody with as much right to it; so is the mail, so is the sword, so was the place in the line, so was the year of instruction from a master-at-arms who had other pupils and gave him the mornings. None of it can be given back, none of it was ever refusable, and there has never before been one thing he could do about any of it. There is today. The far side is going and he goes after it, and then further, past the last of the horns and past the point where anyone behind him could tell him anything. He does not tire. The horse does, and it will not be ridden again, and he comes back at dusk on foot leading it, having finally spent something that was his. Neither desc may name the person at the other end — no name, sex, age, title, birth order or count of the others — and neither may use the words *favourite*, *prefer* or *preferred* or state the comparison out loud, because it is the sort of thing a household knows and never says. Neither may leave the field: no flashback, no room, no table, no farewell at a gate, no letter. The first may not announce a retreat, sound a horn, order a withdrawal in words, be seen by anybody to have changed his mind, consider his own death, weep, blame anybody, or be promised that it works. The second may not be apologised for or condemned, may not think about what he was given, may not be resented or admired by the ranks, may not prove anything to anybody named, and may not fall, be unhorsed or be told to stop — what ends is the horse, not the man, and the page may not treat that as a lesson | 2 templates, base 50, 80 |
| whose oath is the one that broke | The mod asks seven questions about the war and **not one of them is about the oath** — whose war it is, which way it travelled, how long it has run, whether he has another, whether a truce holds across the field, who the participants are. All seven are questions about a conflict. `is_at_war_with_liege` was at zero occurrences and it is the only trigger in the engine that answers the different question underneath all of them: whether the man standing here is in the field against the person he swore to, and is therefore in a position no amount of winning ever entirely undoes. The two gates separate the man who broke it from the man who was brought. The first is in open rebellion himself; the second is not — his own oath is intact and he has kept every part of it — and the lord he swore it to is the one in rebellion, which is how he comes to be on this field with nothing of his own at stake. Both are on the same side of the same battle and the two afternoons have nothing in common. The second carries the first as an explicit negation, because the middle of a three-deep chain coming apart is exactly where both are true at once, and the first wins the tie: a man who has broken his own oath is not usefully described by somebody else's. The second takes the standing guard on `liege`, an optional link — `exists` as a sibling, repeated in the `trigger_if` limit — while the leading `NOT` is a plain read on `root` and can sit outside it. Neither is `bc_fighting_his_lords_war_trigger` or `bc_this_is_his_own_war_trigger`, the near misses: those ask who *owns* the war and are perfectly satisfied by a lord fighting a neighbour on his liege's behalf, while these ask who the war is *against* and only return yes when the answer is the man at the top of your own chain. The commander half is `measured`/`up` and hands out nothing, which is the argument — nothing here braces a line, bleeds it or sets it running, and what changes is only what the man understands he has done. Watching a wing go in exactly where he sent it, it occurs to him that he has been thinking of today as a thing that could still go either way and that this has not been true for some time. There is no version of the next year in which men sit down, agree a misunderstanding got out of hand, and everybody goes back to a slightly worse seat than they had. **That version stopped existing not because anything went badly but because things went well** — every good hour out here has removed one more of the endings in which he simply carries on being what he was, and he has had a great many good hours. He watches the far line give again, entirely calm, with the weightless sensation of a man who has been carrying a decision for months and has just set it down somewhere he cannot get back to. The knight half is `entrenched`/`down`, a rank bracing while it loses, which is precisely what `bc_shieldwall` is for. Not one of the reasons this field exists is a reason of his: he did not want it, was not consulted, gains not a foot of ground from it, and will spend the rest of his life explaining to men who will not believe him that he had no view on the matter. He is not angry — anger needs somebody to be angry at, and the only candidate is a man he is required to obey and has obeyed correctly every day of his adult life, which is precisely why he is here. So he narrows what he is responsible for until it is a size he can be responsible for: a man on his right, a man on his left, and a stretch of churned grass that is going to be given up eventually and is not going to be given up in the next quarter of an hour. The three of them do not move, the rank re-forms on them twice, and neither time does anybody ask him why. Neither desc may name the liege, give his title, house, seat or cause, or state what the rebellion is about; neither may use the words *rebel*, *rebellion*, *traitor* or *treason*, which are words for after said by whoever won, and both of these are happening during. The second may not resent anybody by name, consider leaving, be tempted to, have anybody near him desert on the page, be made noble, be noticed, be rewarded, think about the quarrel beyond knowing it is not his, or use the word *loyalty* | 2 templates, base 50, 80 |
| who made whom | The mod reads the trait and had **never read the relation**. `has_trait = squire` gates `bc_knight_the_squires_best_day` and `bc_knight_what_he_agreed_to`, and both know a man is somebody's squire without knowing whose. AGOT keeps the answer in its own scripted relation — `agot_squire` / `agot_knight`, set in `00_agot_knighthood_effects.txt` and `00_agot_character_history_effects.txt` when a boy is placed with a knight — and it was at zero occurrences here. It is the only record in either game of the one relationship in this setting that is entirely a matter of craft: not blood, not law, not affection, just a man who knew how to do this and a man he taught it to. **The direction is fixed by AGOT and is not the obvious one**: the squire is the character who takes `set_relation_agot_knight = $KNIGHT$`, so on any root `has_relation_agot_knight = X` reads "X is the man who made me", and the reverse read is the same trigger pointed at `root` from inside somebody else's scope. The first takes the standing guard on `enemy_side.side_commander`; the second is an iteration over `any_side_knight`, which is not a link and needs none, and uses `root` inside the entered scope for the reason recorded on `bc_side_has_kin_knight_trigger`. The second carries the first as an explicit negation, because one man can have made somebody in his own line while the man who made *him* commands the far side — a three-generation trade working exactly as it should — and being on the receiving end is the larger fact. Neither is `bc_the_man_who_raised_him_commands_the_far_side_trigger`, the near miss, and the two genuinely overlap in the world since AGOT sets `set_relation_guardian` alongside this one when the boy is under sixteen; they are still different questions, because a guardian is responsible for a child and is finished when he comes of age, while this is a man who taught another man a physical trade and can watch him practise it from the far side of a field and know which parts of it are his. The commander half is `ordered`/`up`, the only KIND in the seam that means *shape* — which is the only thing one man can hand another across twenty years — and the pair that hands out `bc_pursuit`. A section of his line does a thing he did not ask for: a push that could have gone in fast and untidy goes in slow instead, closes up as it goes, takes ground it can still be standing on in an hour, and the men doing it are dressing off somebody without appearing to look at him. He watches it the way you watch a stranger sign a name you know. There is a way of doing that which is not the way anybody teaches it now, which nobody would ever call anything, and which is only distinguishable from the ordinary way if you are one of about four people alive who ever thought about it. **He does not send anyone**, and catches himself opening his mouth to and closing it again, because whatever is going on down there is finished and correct and stopped needing him some considerable time ago. The knight half is `adapted`/`down` and hands out nothing, which is right: none of it alters how the line performs, only how much of himself he has left. The first time it happens he assumes it is luck; the second, good sergeants; by the fourth he has stopped assuming, because the pattern is too tidy — whatever he sets up gets met, not violently and not cleverly but early, in the flat unhurried way of somebody already standing where the thing was going to arrive. So he goes through what he has, in the order it was given to him, which is a horrible thing to notice while you are doing it, and each one is answered before it is finished. **There is nowhere in the whole of him where a thing lives that came from anywhere else**, and by the time the light goes he has understood that he is not being beaten by an army at all. Neither desc may name the other man, give his age, house, title or nickname, or say how long ago any of it happened; neither may use the words *squire*, *knighted*, *dubbed*, *master* or *teacher*, or stage the day it happened — no yard, wooden sword, vigil, shoulder or oath spoken at any age. Neither may have them see each other, call out, salute or know each other's exact position, both moments being about recognising work, which does not require a face. The first may not be proud in words, congratulate anybody, think about his own reputation, have the man he taught be told about it then or ever, or give the order that produces the thing he is watching — the whole weight of it is that he did not. The second may not despair, decide anything about the man opposite, be angry at him, or be shown as outmatched in strength, youth, numbers or luck; he is losing to his own instruction and nothing else, may not invent something new that works, may not be helped, and may not stop | 2 templates, base 50, 80 |
| the cloak and the body it is over | The mod knows who wears the white and had **never asked whose body it is over**. `bc_is_kingsguard_trigger` is the `kingsguard` trait or one of seven council positions and establishes that a man took the vow and nothing else. AGOT keeps the rest of it in a scripted relation — `bodyguard` / `bodyguard_target`, set in `agot_start_bodyguarding` when a sworn sword is actually assigned to a particular person — and it was at zero occurrences here. **The direction is again not the obvious one**: the person being guarded takes `set_relation_bodyguard = scope:councillor`, so it is the sworn sword who ends up holding `bodyguard_target`, and on any root `has_relation_bodyguard_target = X` reads "X is the person I stand over". Neither gate uses `has_relation_bodyguard`. The two gates are the two ends of one arrangement, and the arrangement is not a rank, an oath or an office — **it is a distance**. The first is an iteration over `any_side_knight`, needs no guard and uses `root` inside the entered scope; the second takes the standing guard on `side_commander` and carries the first as an explicit negation, being stood over winning the tie. Neither is `bc_is_kingsguard_trigger` and neither is `bc_knight_pulls_him_out`'s gate, the two near misses: the first of those says a man holds an office while this says which person that office currently resolves to (and AGOT uses the relation for arrangements that are not the Kingsguard at all), and the second is a rescue that happens, while both of these are about a condition that holds all afternoon whether or not anything ever comes of it. The commander half is `ordered`/`down`, shape kept while going backwards, handing out nothing because `bc_pursuit` is for going forward and nobody is going forward. The whole afternoon has been a slow argument he is losing about where things ought to be: the centre is not where he put it, the reserve went in somewhere approximately correct and is now approximately somewhere else, and every correction he has shouted into it has been an estimate and he has known it. Then he turns his horse to look for something behind him and finds, without having gone looking, **that there is one exact measurement left on this field**. It has been behind his shoulder through the collapse of the left, through the reserve, through the two withdrawals he ordered and the third he has not admitted to ordering, and it is not a pace further off or nearer than at dawn. It is the only thing today that has been done properly and it is being done by a man who has not struck a blow. The knight half is `measured`/`up` and also hands out nothing, which is the argument: the most disciplined act on the field changes nothing about how the field performs. It is going beautifully and he can see all of it, which is the difficulty — down in the line a man's world is two arms wide and nobody knows yet that this is a great day, and from where he stands it is obvious. He can name the gap, he knows exactly where he would go and at what moment and what would follow, and there is a long stretch of the day where **the knowing is worse than the not going**, because the knowing keeps arriving in fresh detail and there is nothing to do with it. So he does the work, and the work is being in one place: he shifts his weight when the ground gets soft, he moves when the man he stands over moves, and he does not move otherwise — not when the far banner goes down, not when the cheering starts on the right, not once. The first may not give the order that produces what he is noticing, thank anybody, send the man away, be comforted by him, fear for himself, consider his own death, or turn the loss around. The second may not complain, be tempted in words, disobey by so much as a step, be given anything to do, resent the man he stands over, be noticed, take a wound, see the day end, or use the words *duty* or *honour* | 2 templates, base 50, 80 |
| the marriage that is standing on both sides | The mod had read marriage three times and had **never read the in-laws**. `bc_he_married_into_the_other_side_trigger` asks whether one of root's spouses is close family of the far commander, and there is a pregnancy gate beside it; both go through `any_spouse`. The direction is, for once, the obvious one, but it was checked anyway: it is `every_parent` of the newly married character that takes `set_relation_agot_child_in_law = scope:spouse`, so the parent holds `agot_child_in_law` and on any root `has_relation_agot_child_in_law = X` reads "X married one of my children". **Reading `child_in_law` rather than `parent_in_law` is a distinctness decision, not a taste one**: `is_close_family_of` covers parents, so a gate saying "the far commander is my spouse's father" would be a strict subset of the shipped marriage gate and could not ship — read the other way round it is not, because the man who married root's child is not close family of root's spouse and the shipped gate has never seen him. The second gate is the same arrangement from the younger end, iterated over root's own line, and is not a subset of `bc_side_has_kin_knight_trigger` for the same reason in reverse. It carries the first as an explicit negation: both can be true on one field, and the man across the field is the larger fact and wins the tie. The commander half is `adapted`/`up` and hands out no army modifier at all, which is the argument — knowing a man is not the same as breaking him. The far side does something at midday that nobody around him can read and he reads it immediately, and **the reading does not come from any part of him that was trained: it comes from meals**, from years of watching what that man did with his hands when a dish was late, when a servant spilled, when a conversation went somewhere he had not agreed to go — the small tightening, the pause, then the correction that is always one notch harder than the situation asked for. That is what is happening out there now at the scale of four thousand men. He says nothing about how he knows; he gives the order in the flat voice of a man who has simply decided, and it goes on being the right order for two hours because the far side keeps making the correction he has already watched him make in a hall in winter with the fire going. The knight half is `entrenched`/`down`, one of the three pairs plan 1 called unreachable, and gets `bc_shieldwall` precisely because it is going backwards and refusing to go faster. The line has begun to do the thing lines do before they stop being lines — the men to his left have started to find reasons to be a step further back than they were, each reason a good one, and the sum of them a retreat nobody ordered — and he can feel the pull of it in his own feet. It is not fear exactly, it is the sensible, almost administrative urge to be where everybody else is going to be in a minute. And there is one man thirty paces along the same rank who has known him since before he was worth knowing and who will spend the rest of a long life in the same halls he does, so he sets his feet where they already are and leaves them there, and the shields on either side find him not moving and it becomes fractionally harder for them to move either. Neither desc may name the other man or give his age, house, title, nickname or sex, may name the married child or the spouse, or may use the words *son-in-law*, *good-son*, *father-in-law*, *good-father*, *dowry* or *betrothal*; both pages hold a meal that happened years ago and is still doing work today. Neither may stage the wedding as a scene, go back to it in more than a clause, or describe the contract, the terms, the negotiation or who got the better of it — the arrangement is finished business and only its residue is on this field.
